I’m here because I lost my temper in a way that really bothered me. I was on a board call, and someone asked what should have been a pretty ordinary, small question about the facility budget — nothing outrageous, nothing hostile — and I just snapped at them.

That is not how I run my company. It’s not how I treat people. I’ve spent my whole career keeping things controlled and professional, even when things are tense, and this was… it was out of proportion. I could hear myself doing it, and afterward I thought, “What was that?” It embarrassed me, honestly. It made me concerned enough that I figured I should come in and talk to someone.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not like that, no. I mean, I’ve had tense calls before — anybody in my position has — and I’ve been irritated plenty of times, sure. But I don’t usually lose my temper at someone over something small.

This was a board call, and the question was about the facility budget. It wasn’t even an attack. It was a normal question, the kind of thing I should have been able to answer calmly and move on from. Instead I snapped at the person, and it came out sharp — sharper than it should have, by a lot.

That’s why it stuck with me. I keep thinking, if it had been some major confrontation, maybe I could understand it. But it was such a small thing, and that is just not how I run my company or how I treat people.

Yes, I think so — or at least enough that it caught my attention. I’m not walking around yelling at everyone all day, if that’s what you mean, but that board call was the thing that really made me stop and think something is off.

About six days now. It started after the explosion and fire during that walkthrough.

I don’t know if anxious is the word I’d use. I mean… maybe, in the sense that I’ve felt unsettled, but I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “avoidance” at first, because I keep telling myself I’m just busy or I’ll deal with it later, but… yes.

I haven’t gone back to the new building. I keep finding reasons not to. There’s always some call I need to take, or someone else can check on something, or I can review it from my office. And the paperwork is the same way — the mortgage folder, the safety reports, all of that. They’re sitting there unopened. I know exactly where they are, and every time I think, “Just pick it up, just open the folder, it’s part of the job,” my stomach just drops.

It’s embarrassing to say that, honestly. I’m used to handling these things directly. But right now, anything that brings me too close to that building or the details around it… I just keep pushing it away.

I’m not sure I’d call it guilt, exactly.

I’ve turned it over in my head a lot, but when you ask “do I feel guilty,” I don’t have a clean yes or no. Part of me wants to say, well, I was there, I’m the one in charge overall, so maybe I should feel something like that. It’s more… I keep replaying it and wondering if there was something I missed. But actual guilt? I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve really settled on that.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think there are. I mean, I try not to sit there and picture it, if that’s what you mean. But even more than one single image, it’s all the stuff that connects back to it.

I’ve been staying away from the new building. I tell myself it’s because there are other things to handle, or because someone else can go over there, but if I’m being honest, I don’t want to be near it. And the mortgage folder and the safety reports are just sitting there too. I know I need to open them. I know where they are. But the minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, like my body is saying, “Don’t do this right now.”

So yes, there are thoughts and reminders I’ve been trying not to touch. I don’t like admitting that, because normally I’d just handle paperwork and site issues like any other part of the job, but lately I keep putting distance between myself and anything tied to that explosion and fire.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I lost my temper in a way that really bothered me. I was on a board call, and someone asked what should have been a pretty ordinary, small question about the facility budget — nothing outrageous, nothing hostile — and I just snapped at them.\n\nThat is not how I run my company. It’s not how I treat people. I’ve spent my whole career keeping things controlled and professional, even when things are tense, and this was… it was out of proportion. I could hear myself doing it, and afterward I thought, “What was that?” It embarrassed me, honestly. It made me concerned enough that I figured I should come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of incident happened to you before?\n\nPatient: Not like that, no. I mean, I’ve had tense calls before — anybody in my position has — and I’ve been irritated plenty of times, sure. But I don’t usually lose my temper at someone over something small.\n\nThis was a board call, and the question was about the facility budget. It wasn’t even an attack. It was a normal question, the kind of thing I should have been able to answer calmly and move on from. Instead I snapped at the person, and it came out sharp — sharper than it should have, by a lot.\n\nThat’s why it stuck with me. I keep thinking, if it had been some major confrontation, maybe I could understand it. But it was such a small thing, and that is just not how I run my company or how I treat people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling irritable more often than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so — or at least enough that it caught my attention. I’m not walking around yelling at everyone all day, if that’s what you mean, but that board call was the thing that really made me stop and think something is off.\n\nIt was such a small question, just about the facility budget, and normally I’d handle that in a very straightforward way. I run meetings for a living, basically — I’m expected to keep my tone under control, keep things moving, not take things personally. But I snapped at them, and it came out much more aggressive than the situation called for.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me. It wasn’t how I run my company, and it definitely isn’t how I want to treat people.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling irritable more often than usual?\n\nPatient: About six days now. It started after the explosion and fire during that walkthrough.\n\nI mean, I didn’t wake up one morning just generally irritable for no reason — it feels pretty tied to that. Since then, over these last several days, I’ve noticed I’m quicker to get sharp with people, quicker to react like something is a threat or a problem, even when it’s just a normal work issue. So… roughly six days, give or take, but really ever since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since the explosion and fire during the walkthrough?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if anxious is the word I’d use. I mean… maybe, in the sense that I’ve felt unsettled, but I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way.\n\nSince the explosion and the fire, I’ve definitely felt like I’m not quite myself, but when you say “anxious,” I think of someone pacing around or worrying nonstop, and I’m not sure that’s exactly what I’m describing. It’s more like I’m on edge at times, and then other times I’m just trying to get through the day and not think about it too much.\n\nSo… yes, maybe some, but I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people or places that remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “avoidance” at first, because I keep telling myself I’m just busy or I’ll deal with it later, but… yes.\n\nI haven’t gone back to the new building. I keep finding reasons not to. There’s always some call I need to take, or someone else can check on something, or I can review it from my office. And the paperwork is the same way — the mortgage folder, the safety reports, all of that. They’re sitting there unopened. I know exactly where they are, and every time I think, “Just pick it up, just open the folder, it’s part of the job,” my stomach just drops.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say that, honestly. I’m used to handling these things directly. But right now, anything that brings me too close to that building or the details around it… I just keep pushing it away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the walkthrough?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it guilt, exactly.\n\nI’ve turned it over in my head a lot, but when you ask “do I feel guilty,” I don’t have a clean yes or no. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not in the sense of there being a blank spot, like I’m missing a whole piece of it.

I mean, there are parts I don’t like thinking about, and when I try to go over it too carefully I get tense, so maybe I don’t examine every second of it. But if you’re asking whether I can’t remember important parts, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s not like someone tells me something happened and I have no memory of it.

But I don’t usually lose my temper at someone over something small.\n\nThis was a board call, and the question was about the facility budget. It wasn’t even an attack. It was a normal question, the kind of thing I should have been able to answer calmly and move on from. Instead I snapped at the person, and it came out sharp — sharper than it should have, by a lot.\n\nThat’s why it stuck with me. I keep thinking, if it had been some major confrontation, maybe I could understand it. But it was such a small thing, and that is just not how I run my company or how I treat people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling irritable more often than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so — or at least enough that it caught my attention. I’m not walking around yelling at everyone all day, if that’s what you mean, but that board call was the thing that really made me stop and think something is off.\n\nIt was such a small question, just about the facility budget, and normally I’d handle that in a very straightforward way. I run meetings for a living, basically — I’m expected to keep my tone under control, keep things moving, not take things personally. But I snapped at them, and it came out much more aggressive than the situation called for.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me. It wasn’t how I run my company, and it definitely isn’t how I want to treat people.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling irritable more often than usual?\n\nPatient: About six days now. It started after the explosion and fire during that walkthrough.\n\nI mean, I didn’t wake up one morning just generally irritable for no reason — it feels pretty tied to that. Since then, over these last several days, I’ve noticed I’m quicker to get sharp with people, quicker to react like something is a threat or a problem, even when it’s just a normal work issue. So… roughly six days, give or take, but really ever since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since the explosion and fire during the walkthrough?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if anxious is the word I’d use. I mean… maybe, in the sense that I’ve felt unsettled, but I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way.\n\nSince the explosion and the fire, I’ve definitely felt like I’m not quite myself, but when you say “anxious,” I think of someone pacing around or worrying nonstop, and I’m not sure that’s exactly what I’m describing. It’s more like I’m on edge at times, and then other times I’m just trying to get through the day and not think about it too much.\n\nSo… yes, maybe some, but I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people or places that remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “avoidance” at first, because I keep telling myself I’m just busy or I’ll deal with it later, but… yes.\n\nI haven’t gone back to the new building. I keep finding reasons not to. There’s always some call I need to take, or someone else can check on something, or I can review it from my office. I’m used to handling these things directly. I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve really settled on that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or thoughts about the explosion and fire that you've been trying to avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there are. I mean, I try not to sit there and picture it, if that’s what you mean. But even more than one single image, it’s all the stuff that connects back to it.\n\nI’ve been staying away from the new building. I tell myself it’s because there are other things to handle, or because someone else can go over there, but if I’m being honest, I don’t want to be near it. And the mortgage folder and the safety reports are just sitting there too. I know I need to open them. I know where they are. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been having real trouble sleeping, no. At least, not that I’ve noticed in a clear way.

I mean, sleep is never perfect at my age, you know, and there are nights where I’m lying there thinking about work or whatever else is on my plate, but I can’t honestly tell you, “Since the explosion and fire, I can’t sleep,” or anything like that. So… I guess my answer is no, not particularly. If it’s happening, I haven’t really connected it or noticed it as a major problem.

So… roughly six days, give or take, but really ever since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since the explosion and fire during the walkthrough?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if anxious is the word I’d use. I mean… maybe, in the sense that I’ve felt unsettled, but I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way.\n\nSince the explosion and the fire, I’ve definitely felt like I’m not quite myself, but when you say “anxious,” I think of someone pacing around or worrying nonstop, and I’m not sure that’s exactly what I’m describing. It’s more like I’m on edge at times, and then other times I’m just trying to get through the day and not think about it too much.\n\nSo… yes, maybe some, but I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people or places that remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “avoidance” at first, because I keep telling myself I’m just busy or I’ll deal with it later, but… yes.\n\nI haven’t gone back to the new building. I keep finding reasons not to. There’s always some call I need to take, or someone else can check on something, or I can review it from my office. I’m used to handling these things directly. I don’t like admitting that, because normally I’d just handle paperwork and site issues like any other part of the job, but lately I keep putting distance between myself and anything tied to that explosion and fire.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble remembering important parts of what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of there being a blank spot, like I’m missing a whole piece of it.\n\nI mean, there are parts I don’t like thinking about, and when I try to go over it too carefully I get tense, so maybe I don’t examine every second of it. At least, not that I’ve noticed in a clear way.\n\nI mean, sleep is never perfect at my age, you know, and there are nights where I’m lying there thinking about work or whatever else is on my plate, but I can’t honestly tell you, “Since the explosion and fire, I can’t sleep,” or anything like that. It hasn’t stood out to me the way some other things have.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not particularly. If it’s happening, I haven’t really connected it or noticed it as a major problem.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling less connected to the people or activities you normally care about since the explosion and fire?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / chief_executive / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Inability_Positive_Emotions\nmanifestation: \"Things that usually settle me, like a chess problem, my hiking notes, or dinner with my wife, just feel flat right now, like I know I should enjoy them but I can't get there.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so, though it’s not exactly that I don’t care about people. I still care. I know I do. It’s more like the feeling part of it isn’t coming through the way it normally does.

Like, I’ll sit down with a chess problem, which usually settles me. It gives my mind something orderly to work on, you know, something with rules and a right move somewhere in there. Normally I can lose myself in that for a while. But lately I just look at it and it feels flat. I know I should be interested, but I can’t quite get there.

Same with my hiking notes. I’ve kept those for years, little details about trails and weather and what I want to go back to. Usually that gives me a kind of quiet pleasure. Now I open the notebook and it’s just… paper. Words. It doesn’t lift me.

And dinner with my wife, too. That’s the part I don’t like saying out loud, because it sounds worse than I mean it. I’m there, I’m listening, I’m trying to be present. But something in me feels dulled down. I know I should enjoy just sitting there with her, having a normal evening, but I can’t seem to feel it the way I usually would. It all just feels kind of distant and flat right now.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I lost my temper in a way that really bothered me. It’s not how I treat people. I’ve spent my whole career keeping things controlled and professional, even when things are tense, and this was… it was out of proportion. It made me concerned enough that I figured I should come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of incident happened to you before?\n\nPatient: Not like that, no. I mean, I’ve had tense calls before — anybody in my position has — and I’ve been irritated plenty of times, sure. But I don’t usually lose my temper at someone over something small.\n\nThis was a board call, and the question was about the facility budget. It wasn’t even an attack. It was a normal question, the kind of thing I should have been able to answer calmly and move on from. Instead I snapped at the person, and it came out sharp — sharper than it should have, by a lot.\n\nThat’s why it stuck with me. I keep thinking, if it had been some major confrontation, maybe I could understand it. But it was such a small thing, and that is just not how I run my company or how I treat people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling irritable more often than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so — or at least enough that it caught my attention. I’m not walking around yelling at everyone all day, if that’s what you mean, but that board call was the thing that really made me stop and think something is off.\n\nIt was such a small question, just about the facility budget, and normally I’d handle that in a very straightforward way. I run meetings for a living, basically — I’m expected to keep my tone under control, keep things moving, not take things personally. But I snapped at them, and it came out much more aggressive than the situation called for.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me. It wasn’t how I run my company, and it definitely isn’t how I want to treat people.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling irritable more often than usual?\n\nPatient: About six days now. It started after the explosion and fire during that walkthrough.\n\nI mean, I didn’t wake up one morning just generally irritable for no reason — it feels pretty tied to that. Since then, over these last several days, I’ve noticed I’m quicker to get sharp with people, quicker to react like something is a threat or a problem, even when it’s just a normal work issue. So… roughly six days, give or take, but really ever since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since the explosion and fire during the walkthrough?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if anxious is the word I’d use. I mean… maybe, in the sense that I’ve felt unsettled, but I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way.\n\nSince the explosion and the fire, I’ve definitely felt like I’m not quite myself, but when you say “anxious,” I think of someone pacing around or worrying nonstop, and I’m not sure that’s exactly what I’m describing. It’s more like I’m on edge at times, and then other times I’m just trying to get through the day and not think about it too much.\n\nSo… yes, maybe some, but I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people or places that remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “avoidance” at first, because I keep telling myself I’m just busy or I’ll deal with it later, but… yes.\n\nI haven’t gone back to the new building. I keep finding reasons not to. There’s always some call I need to take, or someone else can check on something, or I can review it from my office. And the paperwork is the same way — the mortgage folder, the safety reports, all of that. They’re sitting there unopened. I know exactly where they are, and every time I think, “Just pick it up, just open the folder, it’s part of the job,” my stomach just drops.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say that, honestly. I’m used to handling these things directly. But right now, anything that brings me too close to that building or the details around it… I just keep pushing it away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the walkthrough?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it guilt, exactly.\n\nI’ve turned it over in my head a lot, but when you ask “do I feel guilty,” I don’t have a clean yes or no. But I can’t point to one specific thing and say, “I did that wrong,” or “this was my fault.”\n\nIt’s more… I keep replaying it and wondering if there was something I missed. But actual guilt? I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve really settled on that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or thoughts about the explosion and fire that you've been trying to avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there are. I mean, I try not to sit there and picture it, if that’s what you mean. But even more than one single image, it’s all the stuff that connects back to it.\n\nI’ve been staying away from the new building. I tell myself it’s because there are other things to handle, or because someone else can go over there, but if I’m being honest, I don’t want to be near it. And the mortgage folder and the safety reports are just sitting there too. I know I need to open them. I know where they are. But the minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, like my body is saying, “Don’t do this right now.”\n\nSo yes, there are thoughts and reminders I’ve been trying not to touch. I don’t like admitting that, because normally I’d just handle paperwork and site issues like any other part of the job, but lately I keep putting distance between myself and anything tied to that explosion and fire.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble remembering important parts of what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of there being a blank spot, like I’m missing a whole piece of it.\n\nI mean, there are parts I don’t like thinking about, and when I try to go over it too carefully I get tense, so maybe I don’t examine every second of it. It’s not like someone tells me something happened and I have no memory of it.\n\nMy memory of it feels… unpleasant, more than absent. Like I’d rather not pull it up, but I don’t think it’s gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been having real trouble sleeping, no. It doesn’t lift me.\n\nAnd dinner with my wife, too. That’s the part I don’t like saying out loud, because it sounds worse than I mean it. I’m there, I’m listening, I’m trying to be present. But something in me feels dulled down. I know I should enjoy just sitting there with her, having a normal evening, but I can’t seem to feel it the way I usually would. It all just feels kind of distant and flat right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you consistently acted out of character or had trouble managing your emotions in situations other than the board call incident since the explosion and fire?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Other than that board call… I’m not sure I’d say it’s been consistent, no. That’s part of why it bothered me so much, honestly. It stood out because it was so unlike me.

I came in because I snapped on that call over a small question about the facility budget, and that is not how I run my company or how I treat people. I’ve been in plenty of tense meetings before. People ask hard questions, sometimes repetitive questions, and normally I can keep my tone even and just answer. That’s the job. But this was… I don’t know, I just went sharp fast, much sharper than the situation called for.

I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m walking around losing control all day or blowing up at everybody. I don’t think that’s true. But that incident made me worry because it felt out of proportion, and it wasn’t the standard I hold myself to.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1, F60.3, F34.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I lost my temper in a way that really bothered me. I was on a board call, and someone asked what should have been a pretty ordinary, small question about the facility budget — nothing outrageous, nothing hostile — and I just snapped at them.\n\nThat is not how I run my company. It’s not how I treat people. I’ve spent my whole career keeping things controlled and professional, even when things are tense, and this was… it was out of proportion. I could hear myself doing it, and afterward I thought, “What was that?” It embarrassed me, honestly. It made me concerned enough that I figured I should come in and talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of incident happened to you before?\n\nPatient: Not like that, no. I mean, I’ve had tense calls before — anybody in my position has — and I’ve been irritated plenty of times, sure. But I don’t usually lose my temper at someone over something small.\n\nThis was a board call, and the question was about the facility budget. It wasn’t even an attack. It was a normal question, the kind of thing I should have been able to answer calmly and move on from. Instead I snapped at the person, and it came out sharp — sharper than it should have, by a lot.\n\nThat’s why it stuck with me. I keep thinking, if it had been some major confrontation, maybe I could understand it. But it was such a small thing, and that is just not how I run my company or how I treat people.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling irritable more often than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so — or at least enough that it caught my attention. I’m not walking around yelling at everyone all day, if that’s what you mean, but that board call was the thing that really made me stop and think something is off.\n\nIt was such a small question, just about the facility budget, and normally I’d handle that in a very straightforward way. I run meetings for a living, basically — I’m expected to keep my tone under control, keep things moving, not take things personally. But I snapped at them, and it came out much more aggressive than the situation called for.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me. It wasn’t how I run my company, and it definitely isn’t how I want to treat people.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling irritable more often than usual?\n\nPatient: About six days now. It started after the explosion and fire during that walkthrough.\n\nI mean, I didn’t wake up one morning just generally irritable for no reason — it feels pretty tied to that. Since then, over these last several days, I’ve noticed I’m quicker to get sharp with people, quicker to react like something is a threat or a problem, even when it’s just a normal work issue. So… roughly six days, give or take, but really ever since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since the explosion and fire during the walkthrough?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if anxious is the word I’d use. I mean… maybe, in the sense that I’ve felt unsettled, but I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way.\n\nSince the explosion and the fire, I’ve definitely felt like I’m not quite myself, but when you say “anxious,” I think of someone pacing around or worrying nonstop, and I’m not sure that’s exactly what I’m describing. It’s more like I’m on edge at times, and then other times I’m just trying to get through the day and not think about it too much.\n\nSo… yes, maybe some, but I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people or places that remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “avoidance” at first, because I keep telling myself I’m just busy or I’ll deal with it later, but… yes.\n\nI haven’t gone back to the new building. I keep finding reasons not to. There’s always some call I need to take, or someone else can check on something, or I can review it from my office. And the paperwork is the same way — the mortgage folder, the safety reports, all of that. They’re sitting there unopened. I know exactly where they are, and every time I think, “Just pick it up, just open the folder, it’s part of the job,” my stomach just drops.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say that, honestly. I’m used to handling these things directly. But right now, anything that brings me too close to that building or the details around it… I just keep pushing it away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the walkthrough?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it guilt, exactly.\n\nI’ve turned it over in my head a lot, but when you ask “do I feel guilty,” I don’t have a clean yes or no. Part of me wants to say, well, I was there, I’m the one in charge overall, so maybe I should feel something like that. But I can’t point to one specific thing and say, “I did that wrong,” or “this was my fault.”\n\nIt’s more… I keep replaying it and wondering if there was something I missed. But actual guilt? I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve really settled on that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or thoughts about the explosion and fire that you've been trying to avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there are. I mean, I try not to sit there and picture it, if that’s what you mean. But even more than one single image, it’s all the stuff that connects back to it.\n\nI’ve been staying away from the new building. I tell myself it’s because there are other things to handle, or because someone else can go over there, but if I’m being honest, I don’t want to be near it. And the mortgage folder and the safety reports are just sitting there too. I know I need to open them. I know where they are. But the minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, like my body is saying, “Don’t do this right now.”\n\nSo yes, there are thoughts and reminders I’ve been trying not to touch. I don’t like admitting that, because normally I’d just handle paperwork and site issues like any other part of the job, but lately I keep putting distance between myself and anything tied to that explosion and fire.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble remembering important parts of what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of there being a blank spot, like I’m missing a whole piece of it.\n\nI mean, there are parts I don’t like thinking about, and when I try to go over it too carefully I get tense, so maybe I don’t examine every second of it. But if you’re asking whether I can’t remember important parts, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s not like someone tells me something happened and I have no memory of it.\n\nMy memory of it feels… unpleasant, more than absent. It’s been about six days now. Basically since the explosion and fire happened during that walkthrough.

I don’t think I made some conscious decision like, “I’m not going back in there.” It was more that afterward, any time something came up involving the new building—going over there, reviewing something tied to it, dealing with those tasks—I found myself putting it off or finding another way around it.

It’s more like I’m on edge at times, and then other times I’m just trying to get through the day and not think about it too much.\n\nSo… yes, maybe some, but I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people or places that remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “avoidance” at first, because I keep telling myself I’m just busy or I’ll deal with it later, but… yes.\n\nI haven’t gone back to the new building. I keep finding reasons not to. There’s always some call I need to take, or someone else can check on something, or I can review it from my office. And the paperwork is the same way — the mortgage folder, the safety reports, all of that. They’re sitting there unopened. I know exactly where they are, and every time I think, “Just pick it up, just open the folder, it’s part of the job,” my stomach just drops.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say that, honestly. I’m used to handling these things directly. But right now, anything that brings me too close to that building or the details around it… I just keep pushing it away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the walkthrough?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it guilt, exactly.\n\nI’ve turned it over in my head a lot, but when you ask “do I feel guilty,” I don’t have a clean yes or no. Part of me wants to say, well, I was there, I’m the one in charge overall, so maybe I should feel something like that. But I can’t point to one specific thing and say, “I did that wrong,” or “this was my fault.”\n\nIt’s more… I keep replaying it and wondering if there was something I missed. But actual guilt? I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve really settled on that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or thoughts about the explosion and fire that you've been trying to avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there are. I mean, I try not to sit there and picture it, if that’s what you mean. But even more than one single image, it’s all the stuff that connects back to it.\n\nI’ve been staying away from the new building. I tell myself it’s because there are other things to handle, or because someone else can go over there, but if I’m being honest, I don’t want to be near it. And the mortgage folder and the safety reports are just sitting there too. I know I need to open them. I know where they are. But the minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, like my body is saying, “Don’t do this right now.”\n\nSo yes, there are thoughts and reminders I’ve been trying not to touch. I don’t like admitting that, because normally I’d just handle paperwork and site issues like any other part of the job, but lately I keep putting distance between myself and anything tied to that explosion and fire.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble remembering important parts of what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of there being a blank spot, like I’m missing a whole piece of it.\n\nI mean, there are parts I don’t like thinking about, and when I try to go over it too carefully I get tense, so maybe I don’t examine every second of it. But if you’re asking whether I can’t remember important parts, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s not like someone tells me something happened and I have no memory of it.\n\nMy memory of it feels… unpleasant, more than absent. Like I’d rather not pull it up, but I don’t think it’s gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been having real trouble sleeping, no. At least, not that I’ve noticed in a clear way.\n\nI mean, sleep is never perfect at my age, you know, and there are nights where I’m lying there thinking about work or whatever else is on my plate, but I can’t honestly tell you, “Since the explosion and fire, I can’t sleep,” or anything like that. It hasn’t stood out to me the way some other things have.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not particularly. If it’s happening, I haven’t really connected it or noticed it as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling less connected to the people or activities you normally care about since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though it’s not exactly that I don’t care about people. I still care. I know I do. It’s more like the feeling part of it isn’t coming through the way it normally does.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down with a chess problem, which usually settles me. It gives my mind something orderly to work on, you know, something with rules and a right move somewhere in there. Normally I can lose myself in that for a while. But lately I just look at it and it feels flat. I know I should be interested, but I can’t quite get there.\n\nSame with my hiking notes. I’ve kept those for years, little details about trails and weather and what I want to go back to. Usually that gives me a kind of quiet pleasure. Now I open the notebook and it’s just… paper. Words. It doesn’t lift me.\n\nAnd dinner with my wife, too. That’s the part I don’t like saying out loud, because it sounds worse than I mean it. I’m there, I’m listening, I’m trying to be present. But something in me feels dulled down. I know I should enjoy just sitting there with her, having a normal evening, but I can’t seem to feel it the way I usually would. It all just feels kind of distant and flat right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you consistently acted out of character or had trouble managing your emotions in situations other than the board call incident since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Other than that board call… I’m not sure I’d say it’s been consistent, no. That’s part of why it bothered me so much, honestly. It stood out because it was so unlike me.\n\nI came in because I snapped on that call over a small question about the facility budget, and that is not how I run my company or how I treat people. I’ve been in plenty of tense meetings before. People ask hard questions, sometimes repetitive questions, and normally I can keep my tone even and just answer. That’s the job. But this was… I don’t know, I just went sharp fast, much sharper than the situation called for.\n\nI wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m walking around losing control all day or blowing up at everybody. I don’t think that’s true. But that incident made me worry because it felt out of proportion, and it wasn’t the standard I hold myself to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been avoiding the new building and related tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s been about six days now. Basically since the explosion and fire happened during that walkthrough.\n\nI don’t think I made some conscious decision like, “I’m not going back in there.” It was more that afterward, any time something came up involving the new building—going over there, reviewing something tied to it, dealing with those tasks—I found myself putting it off or finding another way around it.\n\nBut in terms of how long, yes… about six days. Since that day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I have. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped caring about those things, exactly. That’s what bothers me, because I know I still care. I know chess matters to me, and hiking, and spending time with my wife. But since the explosion and the fire, the enjoyment just isn’t really there.

At least, not that I’ve noticed in a clear way.\n\nI mean, sleep is never perfect at my age, you know, and there are nights where I’m lying there thinking about work or whatever else is on my plate, but I can’t honestly tell you, “Since the explosion and fire, I can’t sleep,” or anything like that. It hasn’t stood out to me the way some other things have.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not particularly. If it’s happening, I haven’t really connected it or noticed it as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling less connected to the people or activities you normally care about since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though it’s not exactly that I don’t care about people. I still care. I know I do. It’s more like the feeling part of it isn’t coming through the way it normally does.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down with a chess problem, which usually settles me. It gives my mind something orderly to work on, you know, something with rules and a right move somewhere in there. Normally I can lose myself in that for a while. But lately I just look at it and it feels flat. I know I should be interested, but I can’t quite get there.\n\nSame with my hiking notes. I’ve kept those for years, little details about trails and weather and what I want to go back to. Usually that gives me a kind of quiet pleasure. Now I open the notebook and it’s just… paper. Words.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Overall, yes, I’d say there’s been a real drop in that.

It’s not that I’ve decided I don’t like those things anymore. That’s what feels strange about it. I can look at a chess problem and know, intellectually, that this is the kind of thing that usually settles me. I usually enjoy sitting with it, working through the positions, feeling my mind focus. But right now it just feels flat. I stare at it and it doesn’t give me that same feeling.

Same with my hiking notes. Normally even reading through trail ideas gives me a little lift, like I’m planning toward something. Now I look at them and it’s just words on a page.

And dinner with my wife… I’m there, and I want to feel present and enjoy it, but it’s like there’s a layer between me and the feeling. I know I should enjoy it, and I want to, but I can’t quite get there right now.

I’m not walking around yelling at everyone all day, if that’s what you mean, but that board call was the thing that really made me stop and think something is off.\n\nIt was such a small question, just about the facility budget, and normally I’d handle that in a very straightforward way. It started after the explosion and fire during that walkthrough.\n\nI mean, I didn’t wake up one morning just generally irritable for no reason — it feels pretty tied to that. So… roughly six days, give or take, but really ever since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since the explosion and fire during the walkthrough?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if anxious is the word I’d use. I mean… maybe, in the sense that I’ve felt unsettled, but I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way.\n\nSince the explosion and the fire, I’ve definitely felt like I’m not quite myself, but when you say “anxious,” I think of someone pacing around or worrying nonstop, and I’m not sure that’s exactly what I’m describing. It’s more like I’m on edge at times, and then other times I’m just trying to get through the day and not think about it too much.\n\nSo… yes, maybe some, but I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people or places that remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “avoidance” at first, because I keep telling myself I’m just busy or I’ll deal with it later, but… yes.\n\nI haven’t gone back to the new building. I keep finding reasons not to. There’s always some call I need to take, or someone else can check on something, or I can review it from my office. And the paperwork is the same way — the mortgage folder, the safety reports, all of that. They’re sitting there unopened. I know exactly where they are, and every time I think, “Just pick it up, just open the folder, it’s part of the job,” my stomach just drops.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say that, honestly. I’m used to handling these things directly. But right now, anything that brings me too close to that building or the details around it… I just keep pushing it away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the walkthrough?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it guilt, exactly.\n\nI’ve turned it over in my head a lot, but when you ask “do I feel guilty,” I don’t have a clean yes or no. Part of me wants to say, well, I was there, I’m the one in charge overall, so maybe I should feel something like that. But I can’t point to one specific thing and say, “I did that wrong,” or “this was my fault.”\n\nIt’s more… I keep replaying it and wondering if there was something I missed. But actual guilt? I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve really settled on that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or thoughts about the explosion and fire that you've been trying to avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there are. I mean, I try not to sit there and picture it, if that’s what you mean. But even more than one single image, it’s all the stuff that connects back to it.\n\nI’ve been staying away from the new building. I tell myself it’s because there are other things to handle, or because someone else can go over there, but if I’m being honest, I don’t want to be near it. And the mortgage folder and the safety reports are just sitting there too. I know I need to open them. I know where they are. But the minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, like my body is saying, “Don’t do this right now.”\n\nSo yes, there are thoughts and reminders I’ve been trying not to touch. I don’t like admitting that, because normally I’d just handle paperwork and site issues like any other part of the job, but lately I keep putting distance between myself and anything tied to that explosion and fire.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble remembering important parts of what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of there being a blank spot, like I’m missing a whole piece of it.\n\nI mean, there are parts I don’t like thinking about, and when I try to go over it too carefully I get tense, so maybe I don’t examine every second of it. But if you’re asking whether I can’t remember important parts, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s not like someone tells me something happened and I have no memory of it.\n\nMy memory of it feels… unpleasant, more than absent. Like I’d rather not pull it up, but I don’t think it’s gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been having real trouble sleeping, no. At least, not that I’ve noticed in a clear way.\n\nI mean, sleep is never perfect at my age, you know, and there are nights where I’m lying there thinking about work or whatever else is on my plate, but I can’t honestly tell you, “Since the explosion and fire, I can’t sleep,” or anything like that. It hasn’t stood out to me the way some other things have.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not particularly. If it’s happening, I haven’t really connected it or noticed it as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling less connected to the people or activities you normally care about since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though it’s not exactly that I don’t care about people. I still care. I know I do. It’s more like the feeling part of it isn’t coming through the way it normally does.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down with a chess problem, which usually settles me. It gives my mind something orderly to work on, you know, something with rules and a right move somewhere in there. Normally I can lose myself in that for a while. But lately I just look at it and it feels flat. I know I should be interested, but I can’t quite get there.\n\nSame with my hiking notes. I’ve kept those for years, little details about trails and weather and what I want to go back to. Usually that gives me a kind of quiet pleasure. Now I open the notebook and it’s just… paper. Words. It doesn’t lift me.\n\nAnd dinner with my wife, too. That’s the part I don’t like saying out loud, because it sounds worse than I mean it. I’m there, I’m listening, I’m trying to be present. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… most of the time, I’d say that’s fair.

It’s not that I have no interest at all, like I’ve completely forgotten these things matter to me. I still know they matter. I still know chess is something I normally enjoy, and hiking plans are something I usually look forward to, and sitting down to dinner with my wife is usually one of the parts of the day that feels good and normal.

But since the explosion and the fire, when I actually try to do those things, there’s just not much there. A chess problem usually settles me down, gives me something steady to focus on, but now I’ll look at it and it just feels flat. Same with my hiking notes — I open them and I know I used to get something out of planning, but right now it doesn’t really reach me.

And with my wife, I’m sitting there and trying to be present, trying to feel the comfort of it, but it’s like I know I should be enjoying it and I can’t quite get there. So yes, most of the time lately, there’s been very little pleasure or pull toward the things I normally enjoy.

So… roughly six days, give or take, but really ever since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since the explosion and fire during the walkthrough?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if anxious is the word I’d use. I mean… maybe, in the sense that I’ve felt unsettled, but I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way.\n\nSince the explosion and the fire, I’ve definitely felt like I’m not quite myself, but when you say “anxious,” I think of someone pacing around or worrying nonstop, and I’m not sure that’s exactly what I’m describing. It’s more like I’m on edge at times, and then other times I’m just trying to get through the day and not think about it too much.\n\nSo… yes, maybe some, but I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people or places that remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “avoidance” at first, because I keep telling myself I’m just busy or I’ll deal with it later, but… yes.\n\nI haven’t gone back to the new building. I keep finding reasons not to. There’s always some call I need to take, or someone else can check on something, or I can review it from my office. And the paperwork is the same way — the mortgage folder, the safety reports, all of that. They’re sitting there unopened. I know exactly where they are, and every time I think, “Just pick it up, just open the folder, it’s part of the job,” my stomach just drops.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say that, honestly. I’m used to handling these things directly. But right now, anything that brings me too close to that building or the details around it… I just keep pushing it away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the walkthrough?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it guilt, exactly.\n\nI’ve turned it over in my head a lot, but when you ask “do I feel guilty,” I don’t have a clean yes or no. Part of me wants to say, well, I was there, I’m the one in charge overall, so maybe I should feel something like that. But I can’t point to one specific thing and say, “I did that wrong,” or “this was my fault.”\n\nIt’s more… I keep replaying it and wondering if there was something I missed. But actual guilt? I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve really settled on that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or thoughts about the explosion and fire that you've been trying to avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there are. I mean, I try not to sit there and picture it, if that’s what you mean. But even more than one single image, it’s all the stuff that connects back to it.\n\nI’ve been staying away from the new building. I tell myself it’s because there are other things to handle, or because someone else can go over there, but if I’m being honest, I don’t want to be near it. And the mortgage folder and the safety reports are just sitting there too. I know I need to open them. I know where they are. But the minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, like my body is saying, “Don’t do this right now.”\n\nSo yes, there are thoughts and reminders I’ve been trying not to touch. I don’t like admitting that, because normally I’d just handle paperwork and site issues like any other part of the job, but lately I keep putting distance between myself and anything tied to that explosion and fire.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble remembering important parts of what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of there being a blank spot, like I’m missing a whole piece of it.\n\nI mean, there are parts I don’t like thinking about, and when I try to go over it too carefully I get tense, so maybe I don’t examine every second of it. But if you’re asking whether I can’t remember important parts, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s not like someone tells me something happened and I have no memory of it.\n\nMy memory of it feels… unpleasant, more than absent. Like I’d rather not pull it up, but I don’t think it’s gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been having real trouble sleeping, no. At least, not that I’ve noticed in a clear way.\n\nI mean, sleep is never perfect at my age, you know, and there are nights where I’m lying there thinking about work or whatever else is on my plate, but I can’t honestly tell you, “Since the explosion and fire, I can’t sleep,” or anything like that. It hasn’t stood out to me the way some other things have.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not particularly. If it’s happening, I haven’t really connected it or noticed it as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling less connected to the people or activities you normally care about since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though it’s not exactly that I don’t care about people. I still care. I know I do. It’s more like the feeling part of it isn’t coming through the way it normally does.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down with a chess problem, which usually settles me. It gives my mind something orderly to work on, you know, something with rules and a right move somewhere in there. Normally I can lose myself in that for a while. But lately I just look at it and it feels flat. I know I should be interested, but I can’t quite get there.\n\nSame with my hiking notes. I’ve kept those for years, little details about trails and weather and what I want to go back to. Usually that gives me a kind of quiet pleasure. Now I open the notebook and it’s just… paper. Words. It doesn’t lift me.\n\nAnd dinner with my wife, too. That’s the part I don’t like saying out loud, because it sounds worse than I mean it. I’m there, I’m listening, I’m trying to be present. But something in me feels dulled down. I know I should enjoy just sitting there with her, having a normal evening, but I can’t seem to feel it the way I usually would. It all just feels kind of distant and flat right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you consistently acted out of character or had trouble managing your emotions in situations other than the board call incident since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Other than that board call… I’m not sure I’d say it’s been consistent, no. That’s part of why it bothered me so much, honestly. It stood out because it was so unlike me.\n\nI came in because I snapped on that call over a small question about the facility budget, and that is not how I run my company or how I treat people. I’ve been in plenty of tense meetings before. People ask hard questions, sometimes repetitive questions, and normally I can keep my tone even and just answer. That’s the job. But this was… I don’t know, I just went sharp fast, much sharper than the situation called for.\n\nI wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m walking around losing control all day or blowing up at everybody. I don’t think that’s true. But that incident made me worry because it felt out of proportion, and it wasn’t the standard I hold myself to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been avoiding the new building and related tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s been about six days now. Basically since the explosion and fire happened during that walkthrough.\n\nI don’t think I made some conscious decision like, “I’m not going back in there.” It was more that afterward, any time something came up involving the new building—going over there, reviewing something tied to it, dealing with those tasks—I found myself putting it off or finding another way around it.\n\nBut in terms of how long, yes… about six days. Since that day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped caring about those things, exactly. That’s what bothers me, because I know I still care. I know chess matters to me, and hiking, and spending time with my wife. But since the explosion and the fire, the enjoyment just isn’t really there.\n\nA chess problem usually settles me down. I like the logic of it, the patience of looking for the right move. Lately I sit there and stare at the board and it just feels flat, like I’m going through the motions but nothing catches.\n\nSame with my hiking notes. I’ll open them and look at trails I used to be excited to plan around, and it’s like I’m reading someone else’s list. And dinner with my wife… I’m there, I’m trying to be present, but I don’t feel that normal comfort or pleasure from it. I know I should enjoy it, but I can’t seem to get there right now.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, overall, you've noticed a significant decrease in the pleasure you derive from activities you normally enjoy since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes. Overall, yes, I’d say there’s been a real drop in that.\n\nIt’s not that I’ve decided I don’t like those things anymore. That’s what feels strange about it. I can look at a chess problem and know, intellectually, that this is the kind of thing that usually settles me. I usually enjoy sitting with it, working through the positions, feeling my mind focus. But right now it just feels flat. I stare at it and it doesn’t give me that same feeling.\n\nSame with my hiking notes. Normally even reading through trail ideas gives me a little lift, like I’m planning toward something. Now I look at them and it’s just words on a page.\n\nAnd dinner with my wife… I’m there, and I want to feel present and enjoy it, but it’s like there’s a layer between me and the feeling. I know I should enjoy it, and I want to, but I can’t quite get there right now.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most of the time since the explosion and fire, you've had little to no interest in doing things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I’d say that’s fair.\n\nIt’s not that I have no interest at all, like I’ve completely forgotten these things matter to me. I still know they matter. I still know chess is something I normally enjoy, and hiking plans are something I usually look forward to, and sitting down to dinner with my wife is usually one of the parts of the day that feels good and normal.\n\nBut since the explosion and the fire, when I actually try to do those things, there’s just not much there. A chess problem usually settles me down, gives me something steady to focus on, but now I’ll look at it and it just feels flat. Same with my hiking notes — I open them and I know I used to get something out of planning, but right now it doesn’t really reach me.\n\nAnd with my wife, I’m sitting there and trying to be present, trying to feel the comfort of it, but it’s like I know I should be enjoying it and I can’t quite get there. So yes, most of the time lately, there’s been very little pleasure or pull toward the things I normally enjoy.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No… I don’t think I’d say no interest at all. That feels too strong.

It’s more like the interest is there somewhere, but it doesn’t turn into anything. I still know those things matter to me. I’ll see a chess problem and part of me recognizes, “This is something I usually like. This usually settles me down.” But when I actually sit with it, it just feels flat. I don’t get that usual pull into it.

Same with my hiking notes. I can open them and remember that I used to enjoy planning routes, thinking about the trail, all of that. But right now it’s like I’m just looking at words and not feeling much from it.

And dinner with my wife… I still want that to feel good. I want to enjoy being there with her. But there’s this awful sense of knowing I should be feeling warmth or comfort, and I just can’t quite get there. So no, not no interest at all — more like the enjoyment is missing, or muted.

Since then, over these last several days, I’ve noticed I’m quicker to get sharp with people, quicker to react like something is a threat or a problem, even when it’s just a normal work issue. So… roughly six days, give or take, but really ever since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since the explosion and fire during the walkthrough?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if anxious is the word I’d use. I mean… maybe, in the sense that I’ve felt unsettled, but I haven’t really sat down and labeled it that way.\n\nSince the explosion and the fire, I’ve definitely felt like I’m not quite myself, but when you say “anxious,” I think of someone pacing around or worrying nonstop, and I’m not sure that’s exactly what I’m describing. It’s more like I’m on edge at times, and then other times I’m just trying to get through the day and not think about it too much.\n\nSo… yes, maybe some, but I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people or places that remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “avoidance” at first, because I keep telling myself I’m just busy or I’ll deal with it later, but… yes.\n\nI haven’t gone back to the new building. I keep finding reasons not to. There’s always some call I need to take, or someone else can check on something, or I can review it from my office. And the paperwork is the same way — the mortgage folder, the safety reports, all of that. They’re sitting there unopened. I know exactly where they are, and every time I think, “Just pick it up, just open the folder, it’s part of the job,” my stomach just drops.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say that, honestly. I’m used to handling these things directly. But right now, anything that brings me too close to that building or the details around it… I just keep pushing it away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the walkthrough?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it guilt, exactly.\n\nI’ve turned it over in my head a lot, but when you ask “do I feel guilty,” I don’t have a clean yes or no. Part of me wants to say, well, I was there, I’m the one in charge overall, so maybe I should feel something like that. But I can’t point to one specific thing and say, “I did that wrong,” or “this was my fault.”\n\nIt’s more… I keep replaying it and wondering if there was something I missed. But actual guilt? I don’t know. I don’t think I’ve really settled on that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or thoughts about the explosion and fire that you've been trying to avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there are. I mean, I try not to sit there and picture it, if that’s what you mean. But even more than one single image, it’s all the stuff that connects back to it.\n\nI’ve been staying away from the new building. I tell myself it’s because there are other things to handle, or because someone else can go over there, but if I’m being honest, I don’t want to be near it. And the mortgage folder and the safety reports are just sitting there too. I know I need to open them. I know where they are. But the minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, like my body is saying, “Don’t do this right now.”\n\nSo yes, there are thoughts and reminders I’ve been trying not to touch. I don’t like admitting that, because normally I’d just handle paperwork and site issues like any other part of the job, but lately I keep putting distance between myself and anything tied to that explosion and fire.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble remembering important parts of what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of there being a blank spot, like I’m missing a whole piece of it.\n\nI mean, there are parts I don’t like thinking about, and when I try to go over it too carefully I get tense, so maybe I don’t examine every second of it. But if you’re asking whether I can’t remember important parts, I haven’t really noticed that. It’s not like someone tells me something happened and I have no memory of it.\n\nMy memory of it feels… unpleasant, more than absent. Like I’d rather not pull it up, but I don’t think it’s gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been having real trouble sleeping, no. At least, not that I’ve noticed in a clear way.\n\nI mean, sleep is never perfect at my age, you know, and there are nights where I’m lying there thinking about work or whatever else is on my plate, but I can’t honestly tell you, “Since the explosion and fire, I can’t sleep,” or anything like that. It hasn’t stood out to me the way some other things have.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not particularly. If it’s happening, I haven’t really connected it or noticed it as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling less connected to the people or activities you normally care about since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though it’s not exactly that I don’t care about people. I still care. I know I do. It’s more like the feeling part of it isn’t coming through the way it normally does.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down with a chess problem, which usually settles me. It gives my mind something orderly to work on, you know, something with rules and a right move somewhere in there. Normally I can lose myself in that for a while. But lately I just look at it and it feels flat. I know I should be interested, but I can’t quite get there.\n\nSame with my hiking notes. I’ve kept those for years, little details about trails and weather and what I want to go back to. Usually that gives me a kind of quiet pleasure. Now I open the notebook and it’s just… paper. Words. It doesn’t lift me.\n\nAnd dinner with my wife, too. That’s the part I don’t like saying out loud, because it sounds worse than I mean it. I’m there, I’m listening, I’m trying to be present. But something in me feels dulled down. I know I should enjoy just sitting there with her, having a normal evening, but I can’t seem to feel it the way I usually would. It all just feels kind of distant and flat right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you consistently acted out of character or had trouble managing your emotions in situations other than the board call incident since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Other than that board call… I’m not sure I’d say it’s been consistent, no. That’s part of why it bothered me so much, honestly. It stood out because it was so unlike me.\n\nI came in because I snapped on that call over a small question about the facility budget, and that is not how I run my company or how I treat people. I’ve been in plenty of tense meetings before. People ask hard questions, sometimes repetitive questions, and normally I can keep my tone even and just answer. That’s the job. But this was… I don’t know, I just went sharp fast, much sharper than the situation called for.\n\nI wouldn’t want to make it sound like I’m walking around losing control all day or blowing up at everybody. I don’t think that’s true. But that incident made me worry because it felt out of proportion, and it wasn’t the standard I hold myself to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been avoiding the new building and related tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s been about six days now. Basically since the explosion and fire happened during that walkthrough.\n\nI don’t think I made some conscious decision like, “I’m not going back in there.” It was more that afterward, any time something came up involving the new building—going over there, reviewing something tied to it, dealing with those tasks—I found myself putting it off or finding another way around it.\n\nBut in terms of how long, yes… about six days. Since that day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped caring about those things, exactly. That’s what bothers me, because I know I still care. I know chess matters to me, and hiking, and spending time with my wife. But since the explosion and the fire, the enjoyment just isn’t really there.\n\nA chess problem usually settles me down. I like the logic of it, the patience of looking for the right move. Lately I sit there and stare at the board and it just feels flat, like I’m going through the motions but nothing catches.\n\nSame with my hiking notes. I’ll open them and look at trails I used to be excited to plan around, and it’s like I’m reading someone else’s list. And dinner with my wife… I’m there, I’m trying to be present, but I don’t feel that normal comfort or pleasure from it. I know I should enjoy it, but I can’t seem to get there right now.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, overall, you've noticed a significant decrease in the pleasure you derive from activities you normally enjoy since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes. Overall, yes, I’d say there’s been a real drop in that.\n\nIt’s not that I’ve decided I don’t like those things anymore. That’s what feels strange about it. I can look at a chess problem and know, intellectually, that this is the kind of thing that usually settles me. I usually enjoy sitting with it, working through the positions, feeling my mind focus. But right now it just feels flat. I stare at it and it doesn’t give me that same feeling.\n\nSame with my hiking notes. Normally even reading through trail ideas gives me a little lift, like I’m planning toward something. Now I look at them and it’s just words on a page.\n\nAnd dinner with my wife… I’m there, and I want to feel present and enjoy it, but it’s like there’s a layer between me and the feeling. I know I should enjoy it, and I want to, but I can’t quite get there right now.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most of the time since the explosion and fire, you've had little to no interest in doing things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I’d say that’s fair.\n\nIt’s not that I have no interest at all, like I’ve completely forgotten these things matter to me. I still know they matter. I still know chess is something I normally enjoy, and hiking plans are something I usually look forward to, and sitting down to dinner with my wife is usually one of the parts of the day that feels good and normal.\n\nBut since the explosion and the fire, when I actually try to do those things, there’s just not much there. A chess problem usually settles me down, gives me something steady to focus on, but now I’ll look at it and it just feels flat. Same with my hiking notes — I open them and I know I used to get something out of planning, but right now it doesn’t really reach me.\n\nAnd with my wife, I’m sitting there and trying to be present, trying to feel the comfort of it, but it’s like I know I should be enjoying it and I can’t quite get there. So yes, most of the time lately, there’s been very little pleasure or pull toward the things I normally enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have had no interest at all in the things you normally enjoy since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’d say no interest at all. That feels too strong.\n\nIt’s more like the interest is there somewhere, but it doesn’t turn into anything. I still know those things matter to me. I’ll see a chess problem and part of me recognizes, “This is something I usually like. This usually settles me down.” But when I actually sit with it, it just feels flat. I don’t get that usual pull into it.\n\nSame with my hiking notes. I can open them and remember that I used to enjoy planning routes, thinking about the trail, all of that. But right now it’s like I’m just looking at words and not feeling much from it.\n\nAnd dinner with my wife… I still want that to feel good. I want to enjoy being there with her. But there’s this awful sense of knowing I should be feeling warmth or comfort, and I just can’t quite get there. So no, not no interest at all — more like the enjoyment is missing, or muted.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… there are some where I still feel a little interest, but it’s faint. It’s not gone completely.

Like with chess, if I see a problem, I still recognize it as something that usually pulls me in. I’ll think, “Normally I’d want to sit with this.” There’s still some part of me that reaches for it because I know it’s supposed to settle me. But then I actually look at it, and it just feels flat. I don’t get that usual feeling of focus or satisfaction.

Same thing with my hiking notes. I still open them sometimes, so I guess that means there’s some interest there. I still know I care about trails and planning and all of that. But when I look at the notes, they don’t give me the same lift. It’s more like I’m going through the motions.

And dinner with my wife is probably the clearest example. I do still want to be there with her. I want to enjoy it. I want it to feel normal and warm the way it usually does. But right now it’s like I’m sitting there knowing I should feel something good, and I just can’t quite get to it.

So yes, some interest is still there, but the enjoyment is really muted. It’s like the things are still important to me, but I can’t feel them properly.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
